After some stimuli escalates one 's awareness of death , the positive reaction is to try to reaffirm that the world has positive aspects as well . " <p> To conduct their research , University of Missouri researchers reviewed dozens of existing studies studies to see if thinking about death can lead to positive attitudes . <p> In one study , American participants were reminded of death or a control topic and then either imagined a local catastrophe or were reminded of the global threat of climate change , with those who were reminded of climate change more likely to express lower levels of militarism than those who imagined a local disaster . <p> Other studies found thinking about death influenced people to make positive choices , including intentions to exercise more , cut down on smoking and increase sun cream use . <p> Even subconscious awareness of death @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who overheard conversations mentioning the value of helping were more likely to help others when walking past a graveyard . <p> Prof Arndt , writing for Personality and Social Psychology Review , said : " According to terror management theory , people deal with their awareness of mortality by upholding cultural beliefs and seeking to become part of something larger and more enduring than themselves , such as nations or religions . <p> " Depending on how that manifests itself , positive outcomes can be the result . <p> " Once we started developing this study we were surprised how much research showed positive outcomes from awareness of mortality . <p> " It seems that people may be just as capable of doing the opposite and ' looking on the bright side of death , ' as the Monty Python song says . @@886590 <h> Ofgem : 95 percent of RHI applicants turned away <p> Ofgem , the administrator for the Renewable Heat Incentive ( RHI ) scheme , has revealed serious problems with the application process after initial figures show that 95 percent of all applicants are returned . <p> The figures outlined in Ofgem 's first quarterly report show that , out of 376 applications , only 20 installations were approved to receive RHI patments . <p> According to the energy watchdog , almost all applications had to be returned because applicants either ; did not contain all the necessary supporting information , did not provide enough information or contained inconsistent information . <p> In waiting for the issues outlined above to be resolved , Ofgem is experiencing significant delays in the process of RHI applications . <p> The main area of confusion for applicants appears to centre on the metering requirements of the scheme . Currently , metering can be classed as either ' simple ' or ' complex ' . If a system is deemed to be the latter , participants will receive payments only on heat used @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ heat used for eligible purposes , and additional meters to calculate what fraction of this heat was supplied by an RHI-eligible source . <p> In order to alleviate the issues plaguing the application process Ofgem has published a Frequently Asked Questions document and committed to providing additional guidance on the level of detail and types of information expected at each stage of the application process . Ofgem " are continually reviewing and improving our IT systems and business processes to further improve and streamline the applicant process . " <p> As of March 31 , a disappointing 5.25MW of capacity had been accredited to the scheme . Of the 20 installs registered , 13 renewable heat installations have been accredited in England , with a further 6 in Scotland and 1 in Wales . <p> The dire success rate of applicants will further compound frustrations of those operating in the renewable heat market after the Department of Energy and Climate Change ( DECC ) announced that it would launch a second phase to the Renewable Heat Premium Payment ( RHPP ) scheme instead of rolling out the domestic Renewable Heat Incentive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is now slated to begin in 2013 . <p> This is not the first time that Ofgem has been criticised for its handling of applications ; the ROO-FiT scheme , also administered by the watchdog , has long been plagued by excessive delays and solar PV installation figures collated by Ofgem have constantly appeared to lag behind actual deployment figures . <p> Ofgem urges all prospective applicants to carefully consider all requirements of the RHI application process before applying to ensure there is no delay in gaining approval . <p> This site uses cookies to store information on your computer , resulting in a better browsing experience for our users . @@888388 <p> For more than 20 years , scientists have been carrying out experiments to mimic the impacts of rising temperatures on the first leafing and flowering of plant species around the world . <p> The bottom line is that the impacts might be bigger than we have believed until nowThis Rutishauser , Oeschger Centre for Climate Change Research <p> Researchers had assumed that plants would respond in essentially the same way to experimental warming with lamps and open top chambers as they would to changes in temperatures in the real world . <p> Very little has been done to test the assumption until this study lead by Dr Elizabeth Wolkovich , who is now at the University of British Columbia in Vancouver . <p> With her colleagues she studied the timing of the flowering and leafing of plants in observational studies and warming experiments spanning four continents and 1,634 plant species . <p> According to Dr Wolkovich , the results were a surprise . <p> " What we found is that the experiments do n't line up with the long term data , and in fact they greatly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ warming , " she said . <p> " So for models based on experimental data , then we would expect that plants are leafing four times faster and flowering eight times faster in the long term historical record than what we 're using in some of the models . " <h> ' Consistent message ' <p> Observational data have been gathered by scientific bodies for many years . In the UK , the systematic recording of flowering times dates back to 1875 , when the Royal Meteorological Society established a national network of observers . <p> Since then , data has also been recorded by full-time biologists and part-time enthusiasts , and in recent years there have been mass-participation projects such as BBC Springwatch . <p> This new research suggests that these observations of flowering and leafing carried out in many different parts of the world over the past thirty years are remarkably similar according to Dr Wolkovich . <p> " In terms of long term observations , the records are very coherent and very consistent and they suggest for every degree celsius of warming we get we are going to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and flower . " <p> She argues that the difficulties in mimicking the impacts of nature in an artificial setting are much greater than many scientists estimate . The team found that in some cases the use of warming chambers to artificially raise temperatures can sometimes have the opposite effect . <p> Image caption National and international citizen scientist efforts will help provide much more data to resolve the question <p> " In the real world , we do n't just see changes in temperature - we see changes in precipitation and cloud patterns and other factors - so certainly when you think about replicating changes in clouds , we are very , very far away from being able to do that . <p> " I guess we will never get to perfectly match nature , but I am hopeful as scientists we can do much , much better , given funding resources . " <p> The team found that the greater investment in the design and monitoring of experiments , the more accurate the result . <p> " We have a very consistent message from the long-term historical records about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more critically about how we fund and invest in and really design experiments , " said Dr Wolkovich . <p> " We do need them in the future , they are the best way going forward to project how species are changing but right now what we 're doing is n't working as well as I think it could . " <p> Other researchers were equally surprised by the results . <p> Dr This Rutishauser is at the Oeschger Centre for Climate Change Research at the University of Bern in Switzerland . He says that in light of this work scientists will have to rethink the impacts of global warming . <p> " The bottom line is that the impacts might be bigger than we have believed until now . That 's going to provoke a lot of work to probably revise modelling results for estimations of what 's going to happen in the future for food production especially . " <p> Dr Wolkovich agrees that if the models are so significantly underestimating the real world observations , there could be also be impacts on water the world over . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ earlier than we expect according to these experiments , it 's going to take up a lot more water over the growing season and if you add to that many years of the model projections , you are going to see big changes in the water supply . " <p> She appeals to people to get involved in citizen science projects and help gather data on flowering and leafing , especially in remote areas . 
@@888485 <h> 02 May 2012 2:03 PM <h> In the interests of security ... but whose interests would that be , exactly ? <p> In the interests of security . It is one of those phrases like ' child safeguarding ' that grates , irritates us all yet can not possibly be contradicted . Being against ' security ' is a bit like being against ' safety ' or in favour of danger . It is akin to supporting terrorists or paedophiles . But something rather worrying is happening and someone needs to do something fast before we travel any further down a very dangerous road indeed . <p> To see where we are going wrong , let 's look at how things used to be , back to the days when anyone who suggested we needed surface to air missiles to guard a sporting event would be put into a lunatic asylum . <p> Back in the early 1990s I lived in Northern Ireland . This was before the Peace Process , before the IRA and the UVF and the rest had put down their guns . Every @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ remember that when the Provos blew up the forensics lab a couple of miles away it felt like WW3 had started . There were shootings , kidnappings and a whole lot of nastiness . These were the dying days of what in reality was a low-level civil war . <p> And yet , the striking thing was that life went on as normal . Yes there were soldiers on the streets and yes , you would occasionally be stopped by a man with a gun and asked to account for yourself or get a cursory search of your bags , but that was that . Once , my wife and I drove up to a hill outside Belfast to see the view , not realising that this was an Army radar station and strictly out of bounds . A soldier appeared out of nowhere , politely tapped on the window of our Mini , pointed out that we were on MOD land and could we please leave . No one screamed at us , or jabbed a gun in our faces . <p> Then there were the airports . I always @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they would not target planes , but even so . This was a place with a large private army on the loose in possession of a couple of tonnes of Semtex ( and the IRA was not the only game in town ) and yet I remember security at Aldergrove and Belfast City to be minimal . No one asked you to take off your shoes or belts . Liquids were fine . There was an x-ray machine and that was that . <p> Today things are so different . As Richard Littlejohn points out brilliantly this week , our police , once a byword for understated professional force , increasingly seem to think they are in a Jason Bourne movie -- dressing up in absurd paramilitary uniforms and each packing enough power to defeat the IRA single-handed . The RUC had a reputation for ruthlessness but really , compared to the hysterical shenanigans we saw on Tottenham Court Road the other day Ulster 's lawmen were like something out of Enid Blyton whose police trundled around in Wolseleys with a bell on the bumper . <p> I remember noisy helicopters @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Now , in London , with no IRA , we have more helicopters than I ever saw over the Ulster skies . What are they doing ? How much do they cost ? <p> After the London Tube bombs in July 2005 we were all evacuated from the Daily Mail offices in Kensington . Fair enough . But outside the police seemed to think they were in the battle of Iwo Jima , screaming at us to move there , move here , red faced -- drunk on authority . I remember the look of bewildered fury on one of the cops ' faces when a colleague berated him for being so rude and told him to calm down . I seriously wondered if my colleague was about to be shot . <p> Compare this to an attack on an office building in Belfast , which took place in late 1992 . This involved a huge bomb which almost destroyed a 15-storey building . I wandered down to have a look . It was chaos , no one knew if anyone had been killed , or if there were more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ police and troops on the scene were calm , unflustered , polite . One constable was happy to point out to me where the bomb had been detonated . There was no shouting , no panic . They did n't even close the road . If this happened today the city would be in lockdown for days . At about the same time a large bomb exploded in the City of London . In 1994 , a major explosion occurred in Docklands . And yet , again , the response was calm , measured . <p> Something has changed . If something like the Bishopsgate or Canary Wharf bombs were to happen tomorrow I can imagine London being paralysed for weeks , swarms of paramilitaries on every corner . The response to any kind of threat , real or perceived , is now one of overwhelming force . Thus when a nutter goes on the rampage we see enough firepower to neutralise a small army . <p> The ' security ' response to even minor events -- motorway crashes , trouble on trains and so on is massive and disproportionate . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the figure quoted is always 14 million but I bet there are a great deal more than that ) . In London you rarely see police on the streets ( there used to be a couple of very pleasant constables who patrolled our local park on horseback , but I have n't seen them for years ) . What you do see are police screaming through the streets at high speed in unmarked cars , usually high-powered Mondeos , discreet blue lights installed in the grille , a scruffy-looking unshaven ' special ' of some kind at the wheel . Again , it 's all very Jason Bourne . <p> We accept assertions of threat without question . We are told we can not take photographs of our children at school sporting events ' because of paedophiles ' . If we make too much of a fuss we worry that we ourselves will come under suspicion . <p> We consent to ever-more-ludicrous examples of what the American author Bruce Schneier calls ' security theatre ' , the whole stable-door-horse-bolted nonsense that is the shoe ritual at airports and the requirement that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ boarding the plane that they are then free to fly into the nearest city if they so wish . <p> We do n't bat an eyelid when the Prime Minister 's wife is subjected to a bag search when she is flying out to Washington on a state visit , as if anyone seriously believed Sarah Brown was going to hijack a plane . It 's in the interests of security , see . <p> What changed everything , of course , were the attacks on the United States in 2001 , reinforced by the bombs of July 2005 in this country and the Madrid massacre of 2004 . Suddenly the new threat of militant Islamism hove into view and the world would never be the same again . <p> Now , this is not an argument that ' we ' , or our security forces , are over-reacting to the Islamist threat . To do so would be to argue from a position of ignorance . People who know a great deal more about this than I do assure me that the threat is not only real but that it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with beards out there who wish to do us harm and our spooks are not only on the case but winning the war . <p> No , what I am arguing against is the collateral damage that arises from the ' War on Terror ' . Calling it this was possibly the most unwise thing George Bush ever did . In its fight against the IRA -- far more of an ' army ' than Al Qaida ever was , with a proper command structure , coherent demands , its own bureaucracy and so forth -- the British government never dignified the struggle as a ' war ' . Indeed , the whole business with Bobby Sands and the hunger strikes arose from the Thatcher 's government 's refusal to treat captured PIRA ' volunteers ' ( in their words ) as prisoners of war rather than criminals . <p> By treating Al Qaida as warriors rather than criminals the War on Terror has reprogrammed our mindset from trusting to fearful . Since 9/11 we have been brainwashed to accept intrusions into our privacy and convenience ' in the interests of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ even when these inconveniences have absolutely nothing to do with Islamist threats and even when we in Britain faced a threat of at least equal magnitude from Irish terrorists as we do now from Islamists . <p> It is because of 9/11 that we can not carry knives onto certain trains ( as if you could hijack a train and fly it into a skyscraper ) and , I would argue , it is because of the ' security first ' mentality that we accept so meekly grotesque intrusions into our personal lives such as the bizarre new rules that mean we can not video our own children in the school play . <p> The wicked crimes that took place on 11 September 2001 have provided an unprecedented boom for the world 's security industry , which was , frankly , in the doldrums in the late 1990s . The Cold War was over and won . There was the hope of peace in the Middle East . The Irish Troubles were nearing their end . Yugoslavia was calming down . If you were in the security business this was a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on Terror changed all that . <p> We now live in a world of surveillance and caution . Again , this is not to argue against the sort of security that foils terror attacks ; the spooks and police need to do their jobs . But I can not believe that putting missiles on East End blocks of flats has anything to do with security at all . What are they going to do with them ? Shoot down a hijacked 747 over Newham ? The attacks of 9/11 are the reason our police have become more aggressive , ruder , less civil and more heavily armed . It can be no coincidence that worldwide the response by civil police services to the various forms of anti-establishment protest that typically accompany economic summits has become increasingly disproportionate . <p> The final , ironic , result of the security insanity is a disproportionate concern with safety by the very people whose job it is to put their lives in danger to save others . Safety is one step away from security and the psychological link between the two explains all those peculiar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climb down wells to save drowning people . It is almost certainly the case that were something like the Twin Towers attacks to happen to a British office block there is no way that our firemen would be allowed to enter the burning building as happened in New York . The irony is , part of the reason for this may be what happened in New York . <p> Timid firemen , aggressive cops veering wildly between over-reaction ( kettling student demos ) and supine passivity ( last summer 's riots ) , creeping , snooping authoritarianism , confusion as to what force is needed , when and to be applied to whom . Idiot councils using ' anti-terror ' laws to catch miscreant parents , ' security ' being the catch-all citation to excuse all manner of official incompetence and pigheadedness . All this is what we get if we lose a sense of proportion and forget to ask the question when told something is ' in the interests of security ' ' in whose interests would that be , exactly ? ' <p> We need to be more strident @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ divulging national security secrets but merely asking on what basis , exactly , has the decision been made to put missiles on London roofs or tanks at Heathrow Airport ? If someone stops you taking a picture of a military base and demands your camera you have only yourself to blame . But if they try the same trick at a railway station or , as has happened , outside Parliament or the Millennium Dome , challenge them . Ask for their ID , and ask them to explain which law , exactly , allows them to do what they are doing . <p> I have always argued that in nearly all ways the modern world is hugely preferable to anything which came before . We are freer , richer , safer and healthier than any humans in history . But a few things have got worse and ' security ' is one of them . Every time you are forced to prove your ID to get on a train , have your tweezers nicked or have your babyfood confiscated at the airport , remember that if the afterlife existed , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <h> Share this article : <h> Comments <p> You can follow this conversation by subscribing to the comment feed for this post . <p> I suggest that the ' tide of insane officialdom ' arises from an excessive faith in the use of regulations to control officials ' actions . Regulations can not possibly cover every circumstance and the official(s) on the scene should be given more discretion to achieve a positive outcome . <p> It is blind adherence to fixed rules that causes bad outcomes : a man dies because firemen may not enter water more than ankle deep , a judge in his courtroom is forced to release an obviously guilty criminal , our Home Secretary may not deport a dangerous terrorist . <p> Most of the time the world works on practical commonsense . Regulations , made with the best will in the world , sometimes are in conflict with commonsense . <p> I am sceptical of rules . Look what happens when trade union members work to rule : everything comes to a grinding halt . Theoretically they should work to rule all the time but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their operation can function . <p> I could n't agree more . We may already have gone too far to turn back this tide of insane officialdom , but a good first step is for as many journalists as possible to keep exposing it for what it is . Thank you , Mr. Hanlon . <p> I must point out that since no-one asked for all this nonsense it is just one more example of our leaders governing us without consultation , a point of relevance to my comment in the " Frankenfoods " thread . <h> Post a comment <p> Comments are moderated , and will not appear on this weblog until the moderator has approved them . @@888496 <h> 02 May 2012 3:59 PM <h> Frankenfoods - the lesser of two evils ? <p> A little more than two centuries ago the British cleric and economist Thomas Malthus made one of the most famous mis-predictions in history . He had noticed that the population of Britain was growing faster than the ability of Britain 's farmers to increase food production . The result , he said , would be famine or war . <p> That this did not come to pass was down almost entirely to technology . Throughout the 19th Century agricultural improvements meant that food production was not only able to keep up with the population boom , it was actually able to outstrip it . Coupled with international trade , the ability of the industrialised world to feed itself became ever more secure into the 20th Century and by the time the US economist Paul Ehrlich made his neomalthusian predictions in the 1960s that we again faced famine , such cassandras were starting to be mocked . <p> But we may have reached the limits of what current technology can deliver . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what it was when Malthus wrote An Essay on the Principle of Population , in 1798 . And it continues to grow . Even the conservative , best-case-scenario predictions see the human tide topping out at between nine and 10bn by the late 21st century , before platueuing and beginning a slow decline into the 22nd and 23rd centuries . The question is , how are we going to get through the next 70 years , which will see a time when there will be more humans alive than at any period before , or probably after ? <p> As in the time of Malthus , we will need technology and this , I am afraid , means genetic modification of some of our main food crops . I am afraid that the threat by the anti-GM campaign group Take The Flour Back to uproot a field of transgenic wheat later this month which has been planted at a crop research institute in Hertfordshire is not only absurd , but obscene -- and profoundly undemocratic . <p> Since the late 1990s , when the first open-air trials of transgenic crops began @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ frankly risible attempts to ' decontaminate ' , or destroy , the plants concerned . The people doing this quote ' public opinion ' or the ' precautionary principle ' to justify their actions . But what they are doing amounts to no more than criminal damage by self-appointed environmental guardians who believe they are speaking on behalf of a public they never consult about anything . <p> Take the proposed ' decontamination ' . The field in question contains a variety of transgenic wheat designed to produce a chemical which protects the crop from attack by aphids . This wheat has not been produced by some sinister multinational , but by scientists at the taxpayer-funded Rothamsted Crop Research Institute , the oldest of its kind in the world . The scientists , who have released a letter this week pleading with the greens to desist , are paid by the British state . As such , their work has a democratic mandate . Destroying this work is akin to breaking into a University library and burning its books . It is destroying knowledge . The sceintists have asked to meet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ temmpted to ask why they are asking to negotiate with criminals ? Bank managers do not ask to ' meet ' bank robbers , after all . <p> Is this what Take the Flour Back wants ? The wheat in question has been created with the developing world in mind . The Green Revolution , initiated by the genius Norman Borlaug in the 1960s , has doubled then doubled again yields of staples such as rice and wheat , using conventional cross-breeding . But we are reaching the limits of what conventional farming can achieve . <p> Think of those figures . Best-case scenario , another 2.5bn humans alive on our planet by 2060 or so . That is an extra China , plus an extra India . And these ' new ' people will not be Chinese or even for the most part Indians ( places where the Demographic Transition has started to kick in , slowing population growth ) but , overwhelmingly , Africans . <p> Africa teeters on the brink of famine . It only takes a limited drought or a minor war for hunger to start to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Kenya . Take one example : the Ethiopian famine which formed the backdrop to Live Aid took place at a time ( 1985 ) when that country 's population stood at 45m . Today , Ethiopia has a population of 90m and a recent study by the US Census Bureau predicted that by 2050 this impoverished nation will be home to 278m people . One of the things that has always struck me about Africa is how empty the place is , despite its image of ' overpopulation ' and teeming masses . Clearly , this emptiness will not last . <p> The reason we see no famine in Ethiopia today is almost entirely due to foreign aid . But how much aid will we need to feed an extra 200m Ethiopians , as well as the rest of Africa 's burgeoning peoples ? ( the population of Niger is predicted to TRIPLE by 2050 , as is that of Nigeria ) This is a continent which can not feed itself today , and according to the UN Food and Agriculture Organisation Africa will be largely responsible for global food demand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , very hard to see how we are going to meet this demand . We can press more land into production of course -- and wave goodbye to the African menagerie as we do so . We can use huge amounts of extra fertiliser , pesticides and herbicides to extract greater yields from the land we do have , at huge costs to the environment . But , most scientists agree , this is n't going to be enough . Sometime quite soon something is going to break and famines are going to break out -- vast , unstoppable famines which will make the Live Aid hunger look like a walk in the park . <p> So , we need a rabbit and a hat , and fast . GM technology is n't the only answer of course . But transgenic technology of the sort being pioneered at Rothamsted promises to be a valuable tool , and denying the right of the world to conduct this research ( and , again , this is state-funded , not-for-profit , unpatented science and the team will allow farmers to access this technology at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ obscenity . <p> Like nuclear power , green opposition to GM is starting to look outdated , hysterical and profoundly anti-human . People like Prince Charles and Zac Goldsmith , champions of ' natural ' organic farming may be well-meaning but they forget they live in a world which can not possibly feed itself even at current population levels using the techniques they espouse . <p> In an ideal world we would not need genetic modification at all . A third think Britain should leave . <p> * Just over half ( 56% ) of the general public believe there should be a referendum on Britain 's EU membership . People over 35 are more likely to support this than younger people . In demographic terms , lower skilled ( C2 ) and unskilled workers ( DE ) are also much more in favour of a referendum -- 62% and 64% respectively -- compared to 48% of white-collar workers ( AB ) . <p> * 68% would be more supportive of Britain integrating further if they thought it would help the UK economy and 57% would be more supportive of EU if Britain was guaranteed interdependence in key policy areas such as taxation . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is strong support for cooperative action on crime and terrorism ( 71% ) , illegal immigration ( 59% ) , and a majority of Britons ( 52% ) think it would be best to use the EU in co-operating and sharing defence resources in order to reduce costs and co-ordinate military action . By contrast , less than half believe it is best to use the EU to negotiate trade agreements with emerging economies ( 41% ) or to set climate change targets ( 47% ) . <p> * There is a great level of concern about the influence of the UK in Europe , which is one of the key conditions people have for giving more support to the EU . But almost half ( 49% ) of Britons believe that staying out of the euro does not limit Britain 's influence in Europe , and 44% think that though it does limit Britain 's influence the benefits of staying out outweigh the costs . @@893470 <p> Every spring is different . Some springs are wet , some too wet , some wet and dry by turns , others plain dry . The ground heats up too early or not early enough or hardly at all . A long cold spell will delay the spring flowers and then in a mad extravaganza they all arrive together . Some springs are good for the apples ; others are better for the plums . Some springs are good for nothing at all . The spring of 2012 bids fair to be one of those . <p> Unseasonably hot weather in March coaxed plants out of their dormancy , but there was so little humidity in the ground that many abandoned the attempt to grow and set about flowering instead . Among them was oilseed rape which began to come on flower when it was barely a foot high . <p> Enter April with the wrong kind of rain . The rain was not heavy and too often wind driven , it was also cold . The airstream driving it was so cold that sometimes the rain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rape flowers remained unpollinated ; no fertilisation equals no seed . No seed , no crop . <p> After the stimulus of the February freeze , apple trees flowered extravagantly , but the flowers were unvisited . The bumble bees had barely time to get airborne before another pulse of cold rain came clattering across the hard ground . <p> In February , these islands were visited by an anticyclone spun out of extreme cold in Russia . On February 11 , a low of -18.3C ( -0.94F ) was recorded at Chesham , which is not in the frozen north but in Buckinghamshire . Less than two weeks later we were 36 degrees warmer . <p> Strange as this may seem , it is not all that unusual . Just as we may have an Indian summer of unusual warmth in autumn , we can experience what our ancestors called " backwinter " , a revisitation of severe winter weather after spring has sprung , so that the young wheat is burnt in the blade . One 17th- century description suggests that backwinter is more likely to come after " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the second half of February , the westerlies took over from the frigid blasts from the Urals and spring began again . Only 13 Februaries in the past 100 have been drier . A rainless March has been followed by the wettest April on record . <p> Although the British are supposed to be obsessed by the weather , they know very little about it . They are certainly aware that their weather is unpredictable but they do n't know why . Forecasters sometimes talk as if a battle were being fought between continental weather systems and the Atlantic . Nobody talks about the phenomenon that is supposed to bring us showers in April , and at any other time of year . Most of us were taught at school that if it were not for the warm Gulf Stream flowing up the western coast of Europe , Britons would be living in the equivalent of Siberia . <p> The Gulf Stream is part of a global circulation system that pushes warm water from the Caribbean into the Gulf of Mexico and up along the Atlantic coast of Florida to pull @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , bringing warm moisture-laden air with it . This is what keeps us so foggy and green that we can make subtropical gardens on the west coast of Scotland . Nowadays it is hipper to call the system by the name that refers to the whole complex -- which involves a complementary movement of colder waters at deeper levels -- as thermohaline circulation or THC . <p> Some people believe that the Gulf Stream has already shut down . They produce as evidence data from the US Navy that appears to show that warm surface water disappears 200 miles off the North Carolina coast . This is itself not evidence that the Gulf Stream has stopped . Oceanic currents can not simply stop , but they can be disrupted and they can change course . <p> The Gulf Stream could be slowing because the salinity of the oceans is decreasing as more fresh water is released from melting ice , but it is thought the cooling effect of such slowing would be more than offset by the rise in global temperatures . If it has the effect of moderating that rise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as good for humans , if not for phytoplanktons and all who feed on them . <p> Another school of thought holds that it never was the Gulf Stream that moderated temperatures on western coasts but atmospheric volatility . Air heated in the tropics expands and pushes its way towards the poles , but being deflected by mountain ranges and suchlike , it rotates away in all directions and brings weather with it . The Gulf Stream section of the THC is also labelled the North Atlantic Gyre and is assumed , reasonably enough , to be wind-driven . <p> Geophysicists working on ocean currents have been so rattled by the possibility of global panic reaction to data that they have yet to unscramble that they have pretty much gone to ground and are saying very little . Scientists can be relied upon to disagree , especially when they are struggling to model phenomena that have never been observed before . Some hold that the behaviour of sea currents is not itself a cause of climatic instability and others , looking at catastrophic falls in atmospheric temperatures over the history of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cap , are disturbed at what appears to be evidence of impending catastrophe . <p> The oceans are still largely unknown to us , and that should cause us more than a twinge of shame . Stephen Hawking talks blithely of how we should be colonising space because within 1,000 years Earth will be uninhabitable either because of climate change or nuclear holocaust . Among the immense treasures that will be discarded when we abandon Earth is the wondrous mechanism of the ocean , trashed before we could understand it . We ca n't build a new Earth if we have n't figured out how this one works .

@@894727 <h> WWF has travelled too far from its original aim , to protect endangered species . <p> What a strange body the WWF ( formerly the World Wildlife Fund , now the Worldwide Fund for Nature ) has become these days . It is the largest , richest and most influential environmental lobbying organisation in the world . Originally set up in 1961 by Julian Huxley , Prince Philip , Prince Bernhard and others , for the admirable purpose of campaigning to save species endangered by human activity , it has morphed in the last 20 years into something very different , more akin to a multinational corporation . <p> The WWF empire now derives a very hefty chunk of its income from partnerships with governments , or the EU , or actual multinationals , such as Coca-Cola and Sky , which like to use its iconic panda logo ( originally designed by the naturalist Peter Scott ) to give an " eco-caring " gloss to their commercial activities . The chief reason why it has so greatly increased its wealth and influence is that it has joined @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Greenpeace , in pushing to the top of its agenda that most fashionable and lucrative of environmental causes , the " battle to halt climate change " . <p> But this has led WWF into some rather odd little tangles , such as those which have recently emerged over its activities in Tanzania . Much of its work there is carried out under a UN climate change policy known as REDD+ ( " reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation " ) , which is part of the UN 's ? 17 billion Fast Start programme . Britain , giving ? 1.5 billion , is that programme 's second largest contributor after Japan . <p> Last November , Prince Charles , as president of WWF UK , flew to Tanzania to hand out " Living Planet " awards to five " community leaders " involved in WWF projects around the delta of the Rufiji River , which holds the world 's largest mangrove forest . Part of their intention has been to halt further damage to the forest by local farmers , who have been clearing it to grow rice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ amounts of " carbon " ( CO2 ) , viewed as the major contributor to global warming . ( Another WWF project in the delta is to find a way of measuring just how great a threat release of that CO2 might be . ) <p> Shortly before the Prince 's arrival , it was revealed that thousands of villagers had been evicted from the forest , their huts in the paddy fields torched and their coconut palms felled . This was carried out by the Tanzanian government 's Forestry and Beekeeping Division , with which WWF has been working . But Stephen Makiri , the head of WWF Tanzania , was quick to insist that WWF had never advocated expelling communities from the delta , and that " the evictions were carried out by government agencies " . <p> At this point , however , two American professors intervened . They had just published a study of the delta in an environmental journal , entitled " The REDD menace : resurgent protectionism in mangrove forests " . It was highly critical of the so-called " fortress conservation " policy advocated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ damaging the traditional life of those local communities which had been sustainably farming and fishing in the area for centuries . <p> Although this provoked a vehement riposte from Mr Makiri , who claimed in turn that the paper had seriously damaged the reputation of his staff who had been working on the WWF REDD project , a new furore had already erupted over claims that some of those staff had been falsely claiming expenses on a massive scale , amounting to more than ? 1 million . <p> In December , WWF responded by commissioning the international auditors Ernst &amp; Young to investigate . In February , it was announced that Makiri had resigned as head of WWF Tanzania . The local office of Britain 's Department for International Development ( DfID ) leapt in to say that , while it " eagerly awaited " Ernst &amp; Young 's report , it wished to emphasise that , although it has a general funding programme with WWF in the area , it had not been responsible for funding any of its projects directly . In March , a statement from WWF @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , confirmed that " so far 13 employees have left the organisation , along with two managers who had oversight responsibility " . <p> When I recently asked DfID what had happened to the report it was " eagerly awaiting " in February , I was told to ask WWF . They told me they had commissioned " a series of reports " on " four projects in Tanzania and the behaviour of a number of staff members there " , not all of which " have yet been completed " . But a summary of their findings will be published " in due course " . <p> It is hardly surprising that WWF is so anxious to defend its good name , since so much of its income ( ? 55 million a year in Britain alone ) depends not just on the five million members it claims worldwide but on the support it gets from governments . Nowhere is this web of top-level influence more striking than in the role WWF now plays in the workings of the UN 's Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change -- the body whose @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ science , have been the chief driver behind worldwide concern over global warming for 20 years . <p> When a series of scandals blew up two years ago over the more alarmist claims made by the IPCC in its 2007 assessment report , the two which attracted most headlines were shown to have been based , not on peer-reviewed science , but on campaigning material put out by WWF . One of these , a prediction that the Himalayan glaciers might all have melted within 30 years , was sourced from a WWF paper based only on a magazine interview with an obscure Indian scientist ( who was subsequently employed by the research institute run by the IPCC 's chairman , Dr Rajendra Pachauri ) . The other , a claim that drought caused by global warming could lead to the destruction of 40 per cent of the Amazon rainforest , was revealed -- by my colleague Dr Richard North and this column -- to have originated in a WWF propaganda leaflet based on research that had not been concerned with climate change at all , but with the damage being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Exhaustive analysis , led by the Canadian author Donna Laframboise , then revealed that nearly a third of the 18,531 sources cited by the report had no more scientific provenance than press clippings , student theses and claims by activist groups -- among which none was more prominent than WWF . But worse was to come . In her recent book on the IPCC , The Delinquent Teenager Who Was Mistaken for the World 's Top Climate Expert , Laframboise shows how , from 2004 on , WWF deliberately set out to recruit contributors to the IPCC 's next report to its Climate Witness Scientific Advisory Panel . <p> The result was that WWF " climate witnesses " contributed to two thirds of the 2007 report 's 44 chapters , including every one of the 20 chapters in the section on the impacts of climate change . A third of all the chapters in the report had WWF witnesses as co-ordinating lead authors , ultimately responsible for their contents . As Laframboise summed up , her analysis confirmed that , far from the report being the work of dispassionate scientists , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ compromised " . <p> Many of these WWF panel members are now at work on the IPCC 's new report , due out next year . WWF has been so successful in getting its allies into key official positions that , in 2007 , the chief executive of WWF UK , Robert Napier , was able to slip seamlessly into a new job as chairman of Britain 's Met Office . This is another body which , through its Hadley Centre on climate change , has been a central player in promoting alarm over global warming ever since 1990 , when the centre was set up by Sir John Houghton , one of the IPCC 's founding fathers . <p> WWF has had only one real setback in its ascent to such influence . In March 2010 , I reported here on its part in a hugely ambitious scheme , backed by $250 million from the World Bank under an earlier version of REDD , to turn the CO2 locked in the Amazon rainforest into " carbon credits " worth an estimated $60 billion . The idea was that these would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ firms in the developed world to stay in business by buying the right to continue emitting CO2 . WWF and others were granted selling rights by the Brazilian government over an area of forest twice the size of Switzerland . But , following the twin failures of the UN 's 2009 Copenhagen World Climate Conference , and the bid to give the US a compulsory " cap and trade " scheme , the project came to nothing . <p> Just how far WWF has travelled from the noble purposes for which it was set up was perfectly symbolised by the way it chose as its chief marketing tool the slogan " Adopt a polar bear " . If this organisation still had concern for endangered species closest to its heart , it would know that the idea that polar bears are dying out due to global warming is no more than sentimental propaganda . But then that is the main business that WWF now seems to be in -- very much at the expense of the rest of us and , of course , those communities in the Rufiji delta .

@@895893 <p> The European Union hosts this week what could be one of the most significant meetings of the year on climate change . <p> Last December 's UN climate summit , in the South African port of Durban , saw heated discussions on a proposal that governments should commit to agreeing a new comprehensive global emissions-limiting deal with some kind of legal force before 2015 . <p> Reluctant nations found themselves up against a burgeoning coalition of principally small countries from the developed and developing worlds alike , which found common interest in tackling climate change as quickly as possible . <p> The rainbow coalition included the EU , the Least Developed Countries ( LDCs ) , small islands vulnerable to impacts such as rising sea levels , and progressive Latin American countries such as Costa Rica . <p> The giant container ships steaming into and out of the Durban docks were matched stylistically by the delegations striding from conference room to conference room , their beetle brows and purposeful gaits testament to the precious cargo they might deliver . <p> And deliver they did , eventually , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2015 and have it in force by 2020 , with every country included . <p> But it was just a promise ; and promises have been broken before on the wheels of realpolitik . <p> Since Durban , real world issues have begun to bear down on those leading the charge towards that new global deal . <p> Recession continues to stalk the eurozone . And even though many European governments say green measures will not impoverish them further and may even make them richer , few are acting as though they believe it . <p> That issue carries over into the climate change discussions , because here too the rich world has promised money - $100bn per year by 2020 - and if pledges are not being met in the arena of overseas aid , why should those developing countries believe pledges will be met in the climate context ? <p> Just five months on , the Durban coalition is a little battered . <p> On Monday and Tuesday in Brussels , at least 30 of the coalition 's key members will meet to re-state their Durban commitment and talk @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the short term to give the 2015 process some momentum . <p> Image caption The next IPCC report is likely to make stronger forecasts on sea level rise than its predecessor <p> They 'll be talking about what needs to be done to ensure that an adequate proposal goes on the table at the next UN climate summit , in Qatar in December , for putting EU emission cuts ( and possibly others too ) under the Kyoto Protocol . <p> They 'll need to discuss how the 2015 deal can bring all countries into a new agreement that will eventually regulate emissions from all countries , yet contains the principle of equity at its heart , allowing poor countries room to emit carbon as they develop . <p> They 'll be trying to navigate the remaining hurdles to fully implementing new international schemes to bring financial support and clean technology from developed countries to their poorer counterparts . <p> And they 'll discuss Rio+20 as well - @@899691 <h> Tim Worstall is a Senior Fellow at the Adam Smith Institute in London , and one of the global experts on the metal scandium , one of the rare earths . His book , Chasing Rainbows , on the economics of climate change , is available at Amazon . <h> Why the answer is a carbon tax <p> The answer is that we should have a carbon tax . So what is the question ? Other than how do we avoid being ruled for ever by Ms Lucas , her sidekick Mr Hines and whatever assorted loonies and ecofascists follow in their wake ? <p> Exercising the Delingpole Clause here ( he can tell you whether it is happening or not ) and just assuming that this global warming thing is going on , what should we do about it ? To which , as I say , the answer is a carbon tax . <p> Firstly , a carbon tax at the level described by the Stern Review -- $80 a tonne CO2-e -- wo n't destroy the economy . Its proper implementation would lower petrol @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the fuel duty escalator has already added some 25p since Ken Clarke ( yes , really ) brought it in to " meet our Rio committments " and actually $80 a tonne CO2 is only 11p a litre petrol . <p> In fact , it 's easy enough to show that a carbon tax would n't in fact raise total taxation very much , if at all . UK emissions are of the order of 500 million tonnes a year , $80 a tonne , that 's $40 billion , call it ? 25 billion among friends* . That 's about what fuel duty currently raises , but we 'll be moving some of that off fuel and on to other forms of energy generation . So we 'll be shifting taxes around but not particularly raising them in total . <p> If you like , we could say that we 're already taxing ourselves enough to beat climate change . Enough , if not quite in the right places . But note that it 's not going to destroy the economy . <p> The other part -- what 's the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on green projects ? -- misunderstands the purpose of the tax . We 're not trying to raise money : we 're trying to change prices . <p> In economic theory , the problem here is that my actions that create emissions also damage someone else . But I do n't have to pay for the damage I 've caused . This is called an externality and the economists ' solution is something called a Pigou Tax . That is , we add a tax equal to the damage I 'm doing , so that I do pay for that damage . <p> We 're not , though , collecting this tax to compensate the bloke being damaged . Nor to extend government , raise MPs ' wages nor fund whatever brain spasm Greenpeace had last week . We 're doing it to change my behaviour . Not to stop me making emissions either , only to stop me making emissions that bring me less benefit than the damage I cause to others . That 's why the tax should be at $80 a tonne , no more . <p> For @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out how we can maximise human utility over time . How can we get the maximum number popping with joy over the centuries ? That means that the benefits to me of my actions have to be measured against their disbenefits to others . Most of the time there are no such disbenefits , or they are already included in market prices . The externalities are the ones that are not yet included . <p> Thus , we want me to stop doing the things where I get less than $80 of benefit for emitting a tonne of CO2 -- but we also want me to continue doing the things where I get more than that $80 benefit . <p> As a made-up example : my car emits one tonne CO2 when I drive it to buy fresh bread for lunch . That 's $80 of damage I cause in the future by doing so . But the benefit to me is trivial : if you paid me 50p ( alright , ? 5 in the rain ) I 'd cycle instead and not emit the CO2 . The value to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ someone else are the $80 . Clearly , this is a bad deal for everyone else : they 're bearing costs much greater than the benefit to anyone at all . An $80 a tonne tax would get me cycling and that would be a good thing : I 've stopped doing something where the benefit is lower than the cost . <p> However , we 've a pregnant woman in pre-eclampsia . She needs to go to hospital in an ambulance which is going to emit that tonne , that $80 worth of CO2 . Without it she and the child will be dead ; with it they 'll be fine . We usually value a statistical life in the ? 2 -- 3 million range . That 's what the railways will spend on safety to save a life on average . Or we could use the ? 50,000 that NICE applies to one year of good-quality life . If your drug treatment costs more than this , then you wo n't get it on the NHS ; less and you might . Different numbers but much the same @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ future damages , because they 're much lower than the benefits that are achieved right now from burning that fuel . <p> In the end , this is what we are trying to do with the carbon tax . We 're trying to embed that calculation into everyday lives . Because we have now changed market prices , we all of us see ( or feel the pain of ) the future damages that our actions will cause . And we 'll only still do those actions where the current benefit exceeds the future damages . <p> The seriously annoying thing about the carbon tax is that we already pay about the right amount of taxes on emissions anyway . We 're just not paying them in the right places . If we were paying them in the right places , we would in fact have already solved climate change . For a properly applied carbon tax is a total solution to the problem . We would have balanced present and future and would end up with the amount of climate change that maximised human happiness . So it is indeed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to p*ss our money away when the solution is already within their grasp . <p> There is one other benefit of the carbon tax . Given that it is a complete solution , once we 've implemented it properly we get to tell Ms Lucas , Mr Hines , all of Greenpeace , FoE , Johnny Porritt and even Chris Huhne to go boil their heads . <p> *A note about numbers . Accuracy can be so tedious but as long as we 've the first digit correct and the number of digits correct this is good enough .

@@901887 <h> Tim Worstall is a Senior Fellow at the Adam Smith Institute in London , and one of the global experts on the metal scandium , one of the rare earths . His book , Chasing Rainbows , on the economics of climate change , is available at Amazon . <h> Why the euro is doomed to fall apart : it was an incredibly stupid idea in the first place <p> The euro is doomed to fall apart : no , not because I 'm some nasty man in UKIP but because the basic idea was such a terrible one . Our chart above ( from JP Morgan Asset Management , which you can see more easily by clicking here ) shows just how terrible it was . It would , in economic terms , have been better to have a new currency for all countries beginning with the letter M than for the eurozone . Or for all countries that have the 5th parallel North passing through them . <p> Yes , of course , we all know , the euro is the bright new dawn , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Again . No one seems to have noticed it that they managed it last time and having experienced the place seem to have no desire at all to go back . So this might not be a problem that needs a solution . <p> However , let 's look behind the political posturing and ask ourselves whether , in economic terms , the euro was a sensible idea . The structure we need to help us decide is Robert Mundell 's concept of an Optimum Currency Area . We should look at things like language barriers , labour mobility , capital , the similarity between economies , their reaction to external shocks -- essentially what has been worked out for us in that chart . <p> And , as you can see , it 's a blitheringly stupid idea to try and push countries into the same currency just because they happen to be next door to each other . People would have been better off if we 'd insisted that the c. 1800 Ottoman Empire had the same currency again : Tunisia , Turkey , Israel and Greece . Which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ try and get Greece and Germany into the same currency . <p> So a very silly thing done by those Very Serious People who have decided they 'd like to rule us . <p> There is one further thing . It is possible to take an area which is not an optimal currency area and make it more so . This is what Lord Mandelson is talking about when he parrots the line about a " fiscal Europe " . The idea is that if the rich areas send money to the poor areas then the effects of being lumbered with the wrong currency can be mitigated . As indeed they can : London sends vast amounts of money to the north of England and Scotland and this helps reduce the impact of the Pound Sterling itself not quite being such an optimal area . <p> Over larger areas and numbers of people , the US is regarded as a good example . However , it is worth looking at quite how much money has to be sloshed around America to achieve a single currency . Some would argue that it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ perhaps plus the military , that does this . That 's about 5 or 6 per cent of US GDP . Others argue that it 's the whole of the Federal government that does it , around and about ( outside current blowout deficit times ) some 20 per cent of the entire economy . <p> Which is fine if that 's your sort of thing . But now try and move this over to the European scenario . The US government , all levels of it , takes some 35 to 40 per cent of GDP . That 's two fifths of everything that everyone produces in a year . And this is after they 've done that fiscal sloshing around to make up for having a single currency . <p> Here in Europe , governments take 40 to 50 per cent of GDP , two fifths to one half , before they 've started to chuck the cash around to pay for the vanity project of the euro . We 'd have to take in tax another 5 to 20 percent of everything produced and ship it off to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a single currency that does n't cripple those poor areas . <p> Five per cent of the UK 's GDP is ? 75 billion or so : that 's more than all council tax and all business rates . Twenty per cent is ? 300 billion : more than all VAT plus all income tax . <p> Can we see any possible future in which we ( or the Germans , the Finns or the Swedes ) agree to pay such taxes so that we can build a Common European Home -- or , if you like , to subsidise the countries which we really should n't have a common currency with ? <p> No , quite . I ca n't , either . <p> Look at that chart again . The UK and its English speaking offshoots : the US , Canada , Australia , New Zealand and so on . They make up a more logical single currency area than the eurozone . The euro was and is an insane economic idea balanced on the nonsense upon stilts of a very silly indeed political ideal . <p> And , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ignore economics does not mean that economics is going to ignore you .

In acknowledgment of his contributions to the plastic theory of structures , and of his research collabo rations with colleagues from the University of Belgrade , Professor Lloyd Smith was elected a foreign member of the Serbian Academy of Engineering Science in 2010 . <p> Alongside his academic work , Professor Lloyd Smith took on a number of responsibilities relating to the teaching and pastoral care of students . In 2000 , Professor Lloyd Smith became a College Tutor . In this role , he was heavily involved in student life across the College , supporting the student experience through his involvement in a variety of pastoral and academic activities . In 2007 , Professor Lloyd Smith was appointed to the newly created post of Dean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the College 's welfare and pastoral care systems , supporting the Pro Rector ( Education and Academic Affairs ) through his work with the student body and the extensive College network of professional support for students . He retired in 2009 . <p> About Imperial College London <p> Consistently rated amongst the world 's best universities , Imperial College London is a science-based institution with a reputation for excellence in teaching and research that attracts 14,000 students and 6,000 staff of the highest international quality . <p> Innovative research at the College explores the interface between science , medicine , engineering and business , delivering practical solutions that improve quality of life and the environment - underpinned by a dynamic enterprise culture . <p> Since its foundation in 1907 , Imperial 's contributions to society have included the discovery of penicillin , the development of holography and the foundations of fibre optics . @@904512 <h> Adapting to climate change in the English Channel <p> Warm water fish from the Mediterranean such as anchovy , red mullet , sea bass and John Dory are shoaling northwards into the south coast 's waters as sea temperatures rise . <p> Bluefin tuna , triggerfish and thresher sharks have also been spotted in the English Channel around Hampshire , the Isle of Wight , and Dorset , with marine scientists predicting a large increase in numbers in the next 20 years . <p> A recent report published by the Marine Climate Change Impacts Partnership ( MCCIP ) focuses on how climate change is creating these new fast-moving ecosystems around the British Isles and what the consequences could be . <p> The UK government is preparing a plan in response to the report to see how the British fisheries and aquaculture industry - worth over ? 1bn - can best adapt to the inevitable changes ahead . <p> It 's like looking out at your bird table and expecting to see sparrows and seeing eagles.Dr Ken Collins , Marine scientist <p> UK Minister for the Marine Environment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ change is having a big impact on distribution of fish stocks and this is going to present some significant challenges for policy-makers , fisheries managers and for the fishing industry itself . " <p> Marine scientist Dr Ken Collins , from the University of Southampton , said the English Channel is a " particularly interesting " example of how climate change is affecting marine life , because it lies between the cold waters of the North Sea and the warmer south . <p> He said : " No doubt the English Channel has warmed up and most of the cod has moved north to cooler waters and we are seeing other marine life moving in from elsewhere . <p> " Bluefin tuna is making a reappearance - but basically the reason that we have n't had much tuna is because we used to overfish it because it is such a popular type of fish . <p> " Trigger fish have become as common as muck . They look very tropical , and 20 years ago they were an occasional visitor , but now we 're at the stage of thinking that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ like looking out at your bird table and expecting to see sparrows and seeing eagles . " <h> ' Fish fight ' <p> Dr Collins , who studies the behaviour and movement of sharks , said thresher sharks are uncommon , but have been spotted in the English Channel . <p> The sharks , more commonly found in warmer waters such as the Mediterranean , are not a danger to humans but divers have been known to be injured after getting in the way of their extremely large tails . <p> Image caption Migrating fish could lead to international disagreements , marine scientists say <p> Scientists are also seeing an increase in the appearance of shoals of anchovy , red mullet , sea bass and John Dory off the south coast . <p> Dr Collins said the British need to adapt to the change in fish stocks and start eating a wider variety of fish , or else the more traditional species of cod and haddock will die out . <p> He said : " The biggest problem is the British public want their cod and chips and it 's hard @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ - would n't it be nice if I tried something else ? <p> " Anything remotely interesting in the way of seafood we tend to ship over to Europe . " <p> The MCCIP report said shifting distributions of fish could lead to an increasing number of international disagreements between foreign fleets , for example fisherman from France and Spain sailing into the English Channel to catch anchovies . <p> Dr Collins said : " The fishing industry have n't got any choice but to change the way they fish and what they fish . <p> " But this can often lead to international disagreements and a bit of fish fight . <p> " The most important thing we can do is to change our fisheries policy and the European fishing policy and stop throwing away perfectly good fish , because it 's absolutely insane . <p> " Most of the fish being caught - sometimes 90% - are just being chucked back into the sea dead . We need to take a more adult approach to things . " 
@@906482 <h> The global cost of China 's destruction of the ' roof of the world ' <h> China 's least talked about crime against Tibet is the damage to the Tibetan plateau : dams , deforestation , mining , poaching and the dumping of nuclear waste . And it is impacting on all of us <p> Much has been written about Tibet under Chinese rule : the religious persecution , the population transfer , the ongoing suffering of the Tibetan people , but I wish to draw attention to the environmental damage the Chinese have inflicted on the plateau . This is a matter that concerns us all . In the same way that the damage to the rain forest affects the people of the entire planet so the damage to the Tibetan plateau has consequences for peoples far beyond the Tibetan borders . <p> During the sixty years of Chinese occupation there has been an ongoing mass damming programme on the many rivers flowing from the Tibetan plateau . The Indus , Mekong , Irrawaddy , Yangtze , Yellow , Salween , Brahmaputra , Karnali and Sutlej @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are vital to millions of people on the Asian continent and sustain the life of , it is estimated , 47 per cent of the world population . The rivers flow into ten countries : China , India , Pakistan , Nepal , Bhutan , Bangladesh , Burma , Thailand , Vietnam , Laos and Cambodia . Recent severe floods in Pakistan , China and Bangladesh has been attributed to the damming of rivers on the plateau . <p> Parts of southern and eastern Tibet are home to some of the best forest reserves in the world . Some of these contain trees which have taken over 100 years to mature , reaching 90 feet . Nevertheless these forests are now being destroyed in the name of ' development ' . Lorries loaded with felled trees roll towards the ' Chinese border ' every day . Tibet had 25.2 million hectares of forest in 1959 . By 1985 13.57 million hectares remained . Now , by China 's own estimate 80 per cent of the Tibetan forests have been destroyed . <p> The Chinese government is aware of the environmental damage @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trying to implement reforestation programmes . But so far - mainly due to the corruption of local officials , who would rather line their own pockets than take any notice of dictates from Beijing - the reforestation initiatives have been largely unsuccessful . <p> Chinese environmentalists themselves have pointed out that the destruction of the Tibetan forests has resulted in soil erosion leading to deposits of silt which in turn leads to the rise of the river beds . The consequence of this , together with the damming projects , has been massive flooding and landslides down stream . The Yangtze flood in 1998 , which claimed the lives of thousands was blamed by President Jiang Zemin on rampant deforestation on the Tibetan Plateau . The frequent flooding that devastates Bangladesh has also been directly associated with the deforestation of Tibet . The recent flooding of the Indus valley , especially severe in Pakistan , is a direct consequence of both over-damming of the rivers and the deforestation on the Tibetan plateau . <p> Before the Chinese invaded Tibet the people lived in harmony with the natural environment . This nature @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ materialistic ideology . The invasion was followed by wide-spread environmental destruction : massive deforestation , overgrazing , uncontrolled mining , nuclear waste dumping , soil erosion leading to landslides and the destruction of many species of birds and animals . <p> Buddhism teaches the interconnectedness of all living things and prohibits the killing of animals . Before the Chinese invasion the animal and bird-life was safe . Not any more . Now the Chinese actively encourage trophy hunting of wild species . Rare animals like the Tibetan snow leopard are hunted for their fur , which is sold on the international market for large sums of money . You can buy a permit to shoot an endangered Tibetan antelope for US$35,000 or an argali wild sheep for $23,000 . Deer antlers , musk , bones and other parts of wild animals are used in Chinese medicine and many animals including blue sheep and wild yak are being poached by hunters to supply meat markets in China . <p> The Tibetan people have suffered more directly from a series of disastrous ' agricultural reforms ' however . Because these ' reforms ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ led to wide-spread famine , unknown before the coming of the Chinese . One such reform forced the substitution of wheat ( unsuitable for the climate ) in place of the barley , which has been the Tibetan staple since time immemorial . <p> Furthermore the intensive agricultural production has resulted in the loss of many medicinal herbs , plants and destroyed winter food supplies . The various ill-thought-out programmes have led to wind and water erosion and desertification . According to the Chinese authorities , 120,000 square kilometres of land in China and Tibet have become deserts as a result of human activity . Nevertheless the interference in the traditional and environmentally friendly farming methods continues . Chinese authorities are still forcing Tibetan farmers to use chemical fertilisers and insecticides . Many of these are harmful to the crops as well as to the environment . <p> The Chinese agricultural policies are not the only ones that make life difficult for the Tibetans still living on the plateau . The once pristine landscape of Tibet is now subject to a series of China 's ' development ' projects . One @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , situated south west of Lhasa . This project is causing a beautiful lake -- sacred to Tibetans -- to dry up . The development of the area has been going on since the nineties and it was early in 1993 that the fresh water springs first became dry . As a consequence the local villagers were forced to drink the water from Yamdrok Tso . It was only when the local people began to suffer from diarrhoea , hair loss and skin disease that they realised the waters of their beautiful lake were polluted . These people have forfeited 165 hectares of their land to a project that has caused them harm . It is estimated that within 20 years the lake will have drained completely . <p> Dumping of radioactive nuclear waste <p> Perhaps even more disturbing is the fact that Tibet has proved an ideal venue for the Chinese government 's development of its nuclear capability . Tibet watchers have known for some time that China has been dumping nuclear waste on the plateau . Only recently have the Chinese authorities admitted it though . Last year it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a twenty square metre dump for radioactive pollutants in Haibei Tibetan Autonomous Prefecture near the shores of Lake Kokonor , the largest lake in Tibet . <p> The first nuclear weapon was brought onto the Tibetan Plateau in 1971 and stationed in the Tsaidam basin , north Amdo . The Tsaidam Basin is an ideal site for nuclear development . It has a high altitude and is isolated . Nuclear missiles have since been stationed at Nagchuka , 150 miles north of Lhasa and recently it was confirmed that there are three nuclear missile deployment sites in Amdo at Large Taidam , Small Tsaidam and Terlingkha . These sites house Dong Feng Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles with a range of 7,000 km . One of the newest missile production centres is located at Drotsang 63 km east of Siling . Anti frigate missiles are tested here on Lake Koklor . <p> The North Nuclear Weapons research and Design Academy is China 's secret nuclear city , adjacent to the town of Halyan in the Haibai Prefecture , Amdo . A doctor who worked at a nearby hospital directly south of the nuclear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ graze near the academy , developed cancer that caused their white blood count to rise uncontrollably . The symptoms were similar to those caused by radiation after the Hiroshima and Nagasaki bombings in 1945 . Many women in this region have given birth to stillborn babies . <p> An underground missile storage centre is located at Payi town in Kongpo Nyingtri , TAR . Local people reported that during mock military exercises a large number of missiles were taken out of the complex , mounted on 20 trucks and fired at pre-arranged targets . There is a lot of talk about ' weapons of mass destruction ' these days , but no one ever seems to mention China in this context , or have I been missing something ? <p> The benefits of Chinese occupation <p> It is too simplistic to assume that the history of the Chinese occupation of Tibet has been an entire disaster for the Tibetans . At various times the Chinese government has tried to win over the people by improving the material wealth of the country . They have increased the average national income , developed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Chinese believe that they liberated the Tibetan peasants from exploitation by a medieval feudal state and a religious establishment . <p> Chen Kuiyuan , who at one time held the position of First Secretary of the Chinese Communist party and was the most powerful man in TAR ( the Tibet Autonomous Region ) , said , ' The central committee of the Chinese Communist party and the State Council have been urging the Chinese people to fully support the rapid development of Tibet and to help the Tibetan people out of poverty and become rich . ' <p> No one disputes the fact that Beijing has poured both money and resources into the Tibetan Autonomous Region . <p> In 1997 Beijing 's outlay for TAR was 3,300,009,776 Yuan . If this is looked at in terms of the population of Tibet , in 1997 Beijing gave every Tibetan , 1,410 Yuan ( $200 ) . <p> In 1985 Beijing initiated 62 construction projects to coincide with the anniversary of the establishment of the autonomous region and in 1995 another 62 projects . The Chinese government point out the economic development of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ any other time in their long history . <p> Tibetan plateau and global climate change <p> But the truth is that all this ' development ' of the Tibetan plateau was got at a price . And it is a price that we all might be compelled to pay . <p> Some environmental scientists believe that the destruction over the last sixty years of the Tibetan environment ( damming of rivers , deforestation , and desertification ) could be impinging on the climate of the entire planet . This is because the plateau , lying at the altitude that it does , is closely bound up with the global jet-stream . When the jet-stream changes course , weather patterns also change . A modification in the jet-stream can have dire consequences . A Pacific typhoon could be one , the El Nino phenomenon , another . As well as altering weather patterns these cataclysmic events stir up the ocean bed and cause disruption to marine life food chains . The livelihoods of many on the north and south America western seaboard depend on the sea so the prosperity of thousands of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by severe flooding in Australia are a direct result of El Nino which also affects the monsoon , vital to South Asia . <p> Once , a land where the environment was pristine , a land where birds and animals prospered , Tibet has been exploited and industrialised by the Chinese to such an extent that the natural environment is ruined , perhaps for ever . Once a peaceful buffer state between India and China , Tibet now is militarised to the point of holding 300,000 -- 500,000 Chinese troops and up to a quarter of China 's nuclear missile force . <p> Once a hidden , isolated , mysterious . A secret land with a unique culture and environment on ' the roof of the world ' . Lhasa , its capitol was the ' forbidden city . ' Now all this is lost and , perhaps , it is not just the Chinese that are to blame . <p> INVESTIGATION Himalayan glaciers are ' not just melting , they are dying'Many glaciers are melting away at a rapid rate . This could have serious consequences for half a billion @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water their crops and for drinking . But as Jonathan Mitchell reports from Nepal , not everyone appears concerned <p> INVESTIGATION A thirst for power : China in TibetSince colonising Tibet in 1959 , China has ripped out virgin forests , dug up minerals and metals , and dumped nuclear waste with little regard for the fragile ecology of the Tibetan plateau .
