@@930835 <h> The joylessness of shopping <p> Somewhat in the manner of a therapist directing his patient towards a profound inner truth , Leo Johnson asked us to close our eyes and recall a time and a place when we we 'd been " totally in the groove , really happy " . <p> We did . <p> Then , he asked how many of us had found that totally groovy happy experience when we 'd been shopping . <p> As far as I could see , no hands went up . <p> " We 've been living a big fat lie , " he said . " We know that consumption does n't make happy lives . " <p> The " green " message , he said , was n't best expressed as " self-flagellation " , about having less ; but about " having more of the stuff we care about " . <p> Several hunded people went along ; and like others I 've been to , the mood of speakers and audience was an amalgam of Obama-style " yes we can " and austerity-era " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that 's quite rational . Anyone aware of what the UK 's chief science adviser Sir John Beddington a few years back termed " the perfect storm " of population growth - growing demand for food and other resources , and environmental factors such as climate change - must logically be optimistic that Rio+20 will do something to change humanity 's unsustainable trajectory . The signals coming from the preparatory talks , however , suggest it 'll be a wiggle rather than a volte-face . <p> Image caption In some poor countries , water supplies are being affected by businesses supplying export goods <p> While much of the Rio agenda focuses on the developing world and people 's rights to water , food , energy , education , health and so on , there are implications for rich Western countries as well . <p> The link is obvious . For example : if a company somewhere in Africa chops down a forest to grow food destined for Europe , and that deforestation wrecks the water supply used by villagers , who 's to blame - the Africans or the Europeans ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ photographer Mark Edwards , a founder of Hard Rain , put it at the EAC event : " We have to change our minds , from wanting as much as we can get of everything to wanting the right amount . " <p> Understandably , not everyone is keen on agreeing measures on sustainable consumption , in Rio or elsewhere . <p> It smacks to many of the " self-flagellation " of which Leo Johnson spoke ; it smacks of interference in people 's freedom to spend their hard-earned cash as they choose . <p> And in these economically straitened times , there 's a conflict with the way many politicians and businessmen see the immediate future and the need to " grow " our way out of recession . <p> Statements such as " we need to boost demand " rarely carry caveats saying that demand ( and the resulting consumption ) should be " sustainable " , or should take note of ecological concerns , or be equitable . <p> I could n't help wondering whether this conflict plays itself out in the Johnson household when they convene for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Finance and a partner with accountancy giants PwC in sustainability and climate change - at one end of the table , and his brother Boris , Mayor of London and a potential future Conservative Party leader , at the other . <p> Does Boris tells everyone how great the beef is to stimulate demand , while Leo takes a small portion and urges sustainable consumption ? <p> You might think that 's a trivialisation of the issue ; but it encapsulates the dilemma facing those Western politicians who care about the developing and natural worlds while also battling recession . <p> In the very early days of preparations for Rio , the idea of discussing sustainable consumption was mooted , even leading to the notion of setting sustainable consumption goals . <p> Image caption President George Bush told the 1992 Rio summit that the American way of life was not negotiable <p> The idea is that these would come into play around 2015 , the target date for most of the existing Millennium Development Goals ( MDGs ) ; but unlike the MDGs , they would be relevant to every country @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be meaningful and comprehensive , many would argue they have to cover consumption patterns in rich nations . <p> One way of squaring the circle would be to measure economic output ( and therefore growth ) in terms other than simple GDP . <p> This would mean taking " natural capital " , the services that nature provides for free , into the equation ; but it would also mean being more acute about distinguishing where and how growth occurs . <p> As a briefing document for another pre-Rio event this week put it : " Increased consumption by those whose basic needs are not met would be considered progress by most . <p> " On the other hand , increasing the competitive consumption of luxury goods among the rich would only be considered progress by a few . " <p> Leo Johnson put it another way , talking of capitalism that did not pursue " the American dream where you serve the same billion people nine times ... rather , you serve the nine billion once or twice . " <p> And Claire Foster-Gilbert , a former advisor to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , had a blunter take : " Could we devise a model of society that did not depend on us becoming ill with our fatness ? " <p> These models exist already - in books , academic papers and the imaginations of many planning to take the road to Rio . <p> The bigger issue is whether Western governments will choose to bring them into reality . 
@@930838 <p> Three senior scientists made impassioned appeals yesterday to anti-GM campaigners not to destroy a field trial of GM wheat which is the culmination of several years ' work . <p> The trial involves a wheat strain modified to be resistant to aphid pests , but an ad hoc group of activists , assembled in a campaign entitled Take The Flour Back , have said they will march to the trial site this Sunday and attempt to destroy the young crops . <p> The activists say the trial , at Rothamsted Research in Harpenden , Hertfordshire , is a threat to agriculture because pollen from the GM wheat could contaminate non-GM plants outside the trial boundary , and they believe GM is in general a dangerous and inappropriate technology for agriculture . If they understand the technology and they understand what we 're doing , then they should embrace it , because really we have the same goals . " <p> Professor Johnathan Napier said : " Why would you want to destroy knowledge ? We ca n't do it by just simple highly-intensive , low-input @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ approaches . " <p> Professor Huw Jones , head of Rothamsted 's Cereal Transformation Lab , said that to destroy the experiment would be " absolutely counter-productive " . He said : " We are going to need to grow an awful lot more food to feed the world by 2050 and to do this more sustainably , with less water and the prospect of climate change , will be a very big challenge . " <p> The demonstrators are meeting at noon on Sunday in Rothamsted Park , and will march to the trial site , which is about a mile away , and which has a 2.4-metre chain links fence surrounding it . " We fully recognise the right to demonstrate lawfully , but it is also our job to uphold the law , and we will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said . <p> The GM wheat plants are now nearly a foot tall , and due to be harvested in September , and the current research consists in measuring the aphid presence in the GM wheat against the number of aphids found in control-plots of non-GM wheat . <p> The modified crops contain a pheromone which is identical to the chemical used by the aphids as an alarm signal ; when the aphids encounter it , they scatter , and aphid predators are attracted . The chemical , E-Beta-farnesene or EBF , is naturally occurring and found in about 400 plants , from hops to peppermint . The current trial at Rothamsted is being sponsored by the Biotechnology and Biological Sciences Research Council , so in effect it is being funded by the taxpayer . <p> In this , as in several other respects , it differs from the widely publicised trials of commercial GM crops promoted by giant agribusiness companies a decade ago , which found they would damage the environment as the extra-powerful weedkillers they were designed to tolerate would kill much other wildlife beside the target pests . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ crops in Britain and in much of Europe , although in other parts of the world GM technology is widely employed in agriculture on crops such as maize and soya beans .

@@931187 <h> Do n't vote Green until they drop the anti-science zealotry <p> * Interestingly , the page on which the Green Party 's Jenny Jones called for the vandalism of scientific research ( linked below ) seems to have gone down . The research is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wheat farming less environmentally damaging : by adding a gene which would make the plants release a pheromone that aphids use to warn each other of danger , the researchers hope that it will reduce pest attacks and thus allow farmers to use less pesticide . Actual supporters of environmentalism , as opposed to kneejerk technophobes , should support the efforts . It is also safe . As Prof John Pickett , one of the researchers on the experiment , said in this paper on Tuesday , fears of cross-pollination of the GM strain with wild plants are founded on a lack of understanding : <p> Wheat is 99 per cent self-pollinating : it is a real challenge to make wheat-to-wheat hybrids , let alone cross it with something else . The only wild plant theoretically capable of crossing to wheat in the UK is couch grass , and although there have been some lab experiments that have made hybrids between wheat and couch grass , this needs human intervention and has never been seen in the wild ... To be doubly sure , we have put in place strict safeguards @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ example by surrounding the GM plots with other wheat and barley crops as a barrier that will all be harvested and destroyed at the end of the trial . <p> So is cross-pollination possible ? Yes , as scientists we work on the principle that anything is possible . Is it likely ? No . What 's more , even if it did happen , the actual chances of this GM wheat successfully establishing itself in the wild are extremely low , since wheat is uncompetitive with other plants . 
@@933190 <h> Tim Worstall is a Senior Fellow at the Adam Smith Institute in London , and one of the global experts on the metal scandium , one of the rare earths . His book , Chasing Rainbows , on the economics of climate change , is available at Amazon . <h> There is no magic money tree of uncollected tax ; we can not close the deficit with more tax inspectors <p> There is joy in heaven and sniggering here on Earth as Richard Murphy gets soundly slapped by HMRC over his lunatic claims that there is a ? 120 billion tax gap . That is , a gap of that size between what should be righteously coughed up in taxes and what is : one so large that if we just employed a few more tax inspectors then we would have no deficit and would n't need austerity . <p> The figure for unpaid tax of ? 28bn that Tax Research UK use is a snapshot of the total amounts owing to HMRC on a particular day . We think this gives a misleading impression of tax @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ within a few days , and over 90pc is eventually collected . Therefore the figure we include in our tax gap estimate shows only the amounts we do n't ever collect . 90pc of this arises because of insolvency . <p> The claim of ? 28 billion shrinks to ? 400 million by the time the adults have looked at it . Do read the whole thing as it takes apart piece by piece most of Murphy 's addled calculations . <p> For example , the method he used to calculate the tax gap from corporations was to look at the headline tax rate -- the 30pc that corporations should pay on profits -- and then look at the actual rate they paid : divide tax paid into profits to get a percentage . As HMRC says and as I 've been screaming for years , this is simply ridiculous . A company using the R&amp;D tax credit , for example ( a 125pc allowance for this sort of spending ) , is not tax evading . It 's not even tax avoiding . It 's doing exactly what Parliament intended @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ true of all sorts of other allowances that are specifically there in the corporation tax code , from capital allowances through to training budgets and any number of greenery plans . He 's counted as tax avoidance things which are simply not tax avoidance . <p> He does much the same with personal taxes as well . And then goes off to use entirely the wrong figures about tax evasion to boot . But you can read HMRC on all of that : Murph makes three howling conceptual errors as well . <p> The first is that he assumes that economic activity which happens now untaxed would also happen if taxed . Which is simply nonsense : it 's ignorant of the most basic economics of taxation . We know very well that taxing stuff reduces the supply . Does n't matter what it is , tax it you 'll get less of it . Trivially , that ? 20 you paid cash to get the windows cleaned might not have happened if it cost ? 40 . But if the window cleaner is to pay VAT and then income tax @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ him the same ? 20 cash in hand at the end of it . Agreed , dirty windows are not the end of the world but you can not go about assuming that currently untaxed economic activity would continue if you were to impose taxes on it . It 's simply ignorant to do so . <p> The second is that nowhere at all does he consider the costs of trying to capture these unpaid taxes . Cost which are a lot more than just some more warm bodies in tax offices by the way . He argues for a general anti-avoidance principle for example : an extreme and horrible breach of the rule of law and thus liberty itself . Quite literally he wants the taxman to be able to say that no , we 're not going to work by what the law actually says . We 're going to work by what we think it ought to say . <p> Which would be interesting in the Vodafone and Cadbury cases : here the argument was whether UK or EU tax law is supreme . EU law is , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have imposed billions in taxes which the law actually said were n't payable . <p> Nor does he consider any of the other costs which might be necessary to reduce tax evasion . Banning cash transactions would obviously aid in reducing tax dodging , but would you really want a society in which you can not anonymously drop a quid into a beggar 's cup ? The truth is that if we 're going to remain a society with any pretence at all to freedom and liberty then we 're going to have to put up with a certain amount of tax dodging . The important question is how much is an acceptable amount of such dodging , not whether there is some left which we must now crush out of existence . <p> Finally , he proposes this increase in taxes as a solution for fiscal austerity . Clearly , he did n't pay attention in his university economics lectures ( as he himself has told us ) for austerity is n't , in this Keynesian sense , about how much the government spends nor about how much it taxes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The more government spending is financed by borrowing , the more fiscal stimulus we have . Even his most basic point , that we should tax more to have less austerity is wrong . For taxing more is , in itself , austerity . <p> So , there is n't a tax gap of ? 120 billion , there are costs to reducing the much smaller tax gap that actually exists and reducing the tax gap will not reduce austerity but will increase it . <p> None of this stops those on the Left parroting his absurdities of course : there are always those willing to believe in the magic money tree .

" <p> ' Natural pest control ' <p> Professor Beddington said that 13 years ago , when protests against GM crops were at their height , there were genuine uncertainties about GM crops . @@937021 <p> The Indus River in Karakoram Range near Skardu , Pakistan , remains a lifeline even in the modern day <p> Climate change led to the collapse of the ancient Indus civilization more than 4,000 years ago , archaeologists believe . <p> The Indus civilization was the largest - but least known - of the first great urban cultures that also included Egypt and Mesopotamia . <p> The empire stretched over more than a million square kilometers across the plains of the Indus River from the Arabian Sea to the Ganges , over what is now Pakistan , northwest India and eastern Afghanistan . <p> Now for the first time scientists believe they have discovered that climate change was a key ingredient in the collapse of the civilisation . <p> The study also resolves a long-standing debate over the source and fate of the Sarasvati , the sacred river of Hindu mythology , the authors believe . <p> Dr Liviu Giosan , a geologist with Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution ( WHOI ) and lead author of the study , said : ' We reconstructed the dynamic landscape of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , built its cities , and slowly disintegrated between 3900 and 3000 years ago . <p> ' Until now , speculations abounded about the links between this mysterious ancient culture and its life-giving mighty rivers . ' <p> Like their contemporaries , the Harappans , who may have made up 10 per cent of the world 's population , the group lived next to rivers , owing their livelihoods to the fertility of annually watered lands . <p> But the remains of their settlements are located in a vast desert region far from any flowing river . <p> The civilisation was forgotten until the 1920s . But since then , a flurry of research has uncovered a sophisticated urban culture with myriad internal trade routes and well-established sea links with Mesopotamia , <p> Archaeologists have also discovered building constructions , sanitation systems , arts and crafts , and a yet-to-be deciphered writing system . <p> Over five years an international team has been combining satellite photos and topographic data to make digital maps of landforms constructed by the Indus and neighboring rivers , which were then probed in the field by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were tested . <p> Co-author Dorian Fuller , an archaeologist with University College London , said : ' Once we had this new information on the geological history , we could re-examine what we know about settlements , what crops people were planting and when , and how both agriculture and settlement patterns changed . <p> Rivers such as the Indus gave life - but when the monsoons vanished , so did the civilisation which relied on it <p> ' This brought new insights into the process of eastward population shift , the change towards many more small farming communities , and the decline of cities during late Harappan times . ' <p> The study suggests the decline in monsoon rains led to weakened river dynamics , and played a critical role both in the development and the collapse of the Harappan culture , which relied on river floods to fuel their agricultural surpluses . <p> The research provides a picture of 10,000 years of changing landscapes and the researchers identified a striking mounded plain , 10 to 20 meters high , over 100 kilometers wide , and running almost 1000 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mega-ridge , ' built by the river as it purged itself of sediment along its lower course . <p> ' The Harappans were an enterprising people taking advantage of a window of opportunity - a kind of ' Goldilocks civilization , ' said Dr Giosan . <p> Seat of power : The empire stretched from the Arabian Sea to the Ganges , over what is now Pakistan , northwest India and eastern Afghanistan <p> ' As monsoon drying subdued devastating floods , the land nearby the rivers - still fed with water and rich silt - was just right for agriculture . This lasted for almost 2,000 years , but continued aridification closed this favorable window in the end . ' <p> The researchers believe they have uncovered the fate of a mythical river , the Sarasvati , described in The Vedas , ancient Indian scriptures composed in Sanskrit over 3000 years ago , which it is believed was fed by perennial glaciers in the Himalayas . <p> Today , the Ghaggar , an intermittent river that flows only during strong monsoons and dissipates into the desert along the dried course @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ location of the mythic Sarasvati , but its Himalayan origin and whether it was active during Vedic times remain controversial . <p> By 3900 years ago , their rivers drying , the Harappans had an escape route to the east toward the Ganges basin , where monsoon rains remained reliable . <p> ' We can envision that this eastern shift involved a change to more localised forms of economy : smaller communities supported by local rain-fed farming and dwindling streams , ' said Dr Fuller . <p> ' This may have produced smaller surpluses , and would not have supported large cities , but would have been reliable . <p> ' Cities collapsed , but smaller agricultural communities were sustainable and flourished . Many of the urban arts , such as writing , faded away , but agriculture continued and actually diversified . ' <p> Dr Giosan added : ' An amazing amount of archaeological work has been accumulating over the last decades , but it 's never been linked properly to the evolution of the fluvial landscape . We now see landscape dynamics as the crucial link between climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the largest irrigation scheme in the world , immobilizing the river in channels and behind dams . If the monsoon were to increase in a warming world , as some predict , catastrophic floods such as the humanitarian disaster of 2010 , would turn the current irrigation system , designed for a tamer river , obsolete . @@937129 <h> Earth took ten million years to recover from Permian-Triassic extinction <p> Only one in ten species of plant and animal survived the largest-known , most-catastrophic die-off of life on Earth , which is thought to have been the only mass extinction event to have significantly affected insects . Marine species were hit worst , with 96 percent dying . Sea scorpions , trilobites and certain types of starfish were permanently made extinct , and only two and three percent of snails and squid survived , respectively . <p> A review in Nature Geosciences from Zhong-Qiang Chen , of the China University of Geosciences in Wuhan , and Michael Benton of the University of Bristol , concludes that it took ten million years before the Earth was able to fully recover . <p> The disaster , known as the Permian-Triassic extinction event and referred to by Nasa as " The Great Dying " , occurred when the Earth 's continents were mashed-up into one landmass , known as Pangaea . It 's thought to have been triggered by a combination of climate change , acid rain , ocean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of rock sections found in China and across the world to try and get an idea of the timeline of extinction . They claim that after the initial cataclysm , grim conditions returned in bursts for five or six million years with repeated carbon and oxygen crises , global warming , and other nasty events . <p> Animals that did manage to recover reasonably quickly and rebuild their ecosystems were hit repeatedly , and as a result , permanent ecosystems took around five million years to re-establish . Benton said : " Life seemed to be getting back to normal when another crisis hit and set it back again . The carbon crises were repeated many times , and then finally conditions became normal again after five million years or so . " <p> It was only after the environmental shocks eased that complex ecosystems were able to re-emerge . The species that emerged from the events formed the basis of modern-style ecosystems -- consisting in the sea of ancestral crabs , lobsters , and the first marine reptiles , and on the land of tetrapods which eventually became the dinosaurs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ imagine how so much of life could have been killed , but there is no doubt from some of the fantastic rock sections in China and elsewhere round the world that this was the biggest crisis ever faced by life . " <p> Benton added : " We often see mass extinctions as entirely negative but in this most devastating case , life did recover , after many millions of years , and new groups emerged . The event had re-set evolution . However , the causes of the killing -- global warming , acid rain , ocean acidification -- sound eerily familiar to us today . Perhaps we can learn something from these ancient events . "

@@939722 <h> Recent Articles <p> A US government-funded survey has found that Americans with higher levels of scientific and mathematical knowledge are more sceptical regarding the dangers of climate change than their more poorly educated fellow citizens . <p> The results of the survey are especially remarkable as it was plainly not intended to show any such thing : Rather , the researchers and trick-cyclists who carried it out were doing so from the position that the " scientific consensus " ( carbon-driven global warming is ongoing and extremely dangerous ) is a settled fact , and the priority is now to find some way of getting US voters to believe in the need for urgent , immediate and massive action to reduce CO2 emissions . <p> A theory exists among some psychologists , sociologists and other soft " scientists " that it should be possible to convince the ordinary citizenry to accept the various huge costs advocated by environmentalists , by simply raising the level of scientific knowledge and numeracy . People would then be able to understand that there is a terrible danger facing the human race @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it appears to be a fact that very few people in the general public -- or indeed , in various architecture and industrial-design faculties -- have enough basic physics and numeracy to join the debate at all ( as the recent rash of human-powered " crowd farm " generator projects illustrates all too plainly ) . <p> Thus , in a just-published US National Science Foundation-funded study , participants ' science knowledge and numeracy was tested and compared with levels of concern regarding climate change . The soft-studies profs were amazed , however , to find that as one moves up the scale of science knowledge and numeracy , people become more sceptical , not less . <p> According to the profs , this is not because the idea of imminent carbon-driven catastrophe is perhaps a bit scientifically suspect . Rather it is because people classed as " egalitarian communitarians " ( roughly speaking , left-wingers ) are always highly concerned about climate change , and become slightly more so as they acquire more science and numeracy . Unfortunately , however , " hierarchical individualists " ( basically , right-wingers ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : but if they have any scientific , mathematic or technical education this causes them to become strongly sceptical . <p> As scientific/tech knowledge and numeracy appears to be more common among " hierarchical individualists " than among " egalitarian communitarians " , this meant that in the sample as a whole the effect of more scientific knowledge and numeracy was to increase scepticism . <p> Given that the profs had assumed from the start that scepticism is wrong , this forced them to the conclusion that simply teaching people more science and giving them more facts and numbers is not a good idea as it will lead them into bad ( sceptical ) decisions . They write : <p> This form of reasoning can have a highly negative impact on collective decision making ... it is very harmful to collective welfare for individuals in aggregate to form beliefs this way . <p> One aim of science communication , we submit , should be to dispel this tragedy ... A communication strategy that focuses only on transmission of sound scientific information , our results suggest , is unlikely to do that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the clarity of scientific information will not dispel public conflict ... <p> Thus it is , according to the assembled profs , that the US government should seek to fund a communication strategy on climate change which is not focused on sound scientific information . <p> It does not follow , however , that nothing can be done ... Effective strategies include use of culturally diverse communicators , whose affinity with different communities enhances their credibility , and information-framing techniques that invest policy solutions with resonances congenial to diverse groups . Perfecting such techniques through a new science of science communication is a public good of singular importance . <p> There 's something of a push developing in psychology , sociology and other soft-science departments worldwide for this " new science of communicating science " , which is supposed to galvanise the electorates of the developed world into demanding serious action against carbon emissions and acceptance of the serious sacrifices this would mean . We 've compared this idea before to Isaac Asimov 's science-fictional notion of the discipline of " Psychohistory " - a set of methods which could be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This might be seen from an egalitarian communitarian viewpoint as a good thing , simply changing people 's beliefs for the manifest collective good . <p> Your hierarchical individualist , however , might sneer cynically -- first at the prospect of a shower of trick-cyclists managing to change his or her mind on climate change by means of spin rather than hard numbers . The hierarchical individualist might also view the " science of communicating science " push as a rather ignoble attempt by the soft-studies profs to get a share of the climate change research funding bonanza that has poured into the hard science and biology faculties in recent decades . <p> And anyone at all might be rather alarmed , perhaps , at the prospect of actual success in the matter of developing a working discipline of Psychohistory -- which could and would surely be used in other areas than climate change policy , and would surely be a threat to democracy if it worked as advertised . <p> For all that there 's no serious likelihood of the soft-studies profs genuinely managing to pump up climate fear successfully @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , US taxpayers of every political stripe might very well quarrel with the idea of spending their science budget with the aim of placing enormous political power in the hands of the trick-cyclist community . <h> Bootnote <p> *Though in the Foundation saga this was only possible with vast galactic populations of the far future , with humans as numerous as gas molecules in a pressure vessel , and even then it was necessary to keep the existence of Psychohistory a secret .

@@941883 <p> Slowly the clicks of the motor drives die down until there is silence in that sunny glade , save for the sounds of prehistory : birdsong and breeze in the trees . Something is going on here , something like species-bonding . We humans are looking in a mirror tens of millions of years old , rendered speechless by what we see . <p> Above us , fellow primates whizz through the rainforest canopy . They have not yet learnt to walk as we walk . It 'll be tens of millions more years before they manage that . From down here you wonder why they 'll bother -- tree life , as lived by the indri , looks like a laugh a minute . But looks can be deceptive . <p> The fluffy black-and-white indri are among the most captivating of the 80 or so lemur species of Madagascar . They fly through the trees like Cirque du Soleil performers in panda outfits , they tumble and tease with dizzying weightlessness . But , after all those millions of years , their continued existence is now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- though hunting and climate change are also to blame -- the indri population , unique to the rainforests of eastern Madagascar , is thought to have halved in the past 40 years . No one knows for sure , but it could be as low as 1,000 . <p> The group we came across , a family of four , lives in Andasibe National Park , 2,000 acres of montane rainforest about 90 miles east of the Malagasy capital , Antananarivo ( commonly known as Tana ) . Once their famously loud and heart-rending call echoed for a thousand miles through the vertical belt of rainforest occupying Madagascar 's easternmost quarter . Now it is confined to pockets such as Andasibe . <p> The communication call of the indri is one of the unforgettable sounds of nature . Perhaps the woman wailing for her demon lover in Coleridge 's Kubla Khan would sound like this -- certainly nothing else on Earth does . The indri opens its mouth , and heedless of the terror and wonder it is about to engender in the human breast , issues a siren-like howl @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tree canopy . <p> We first heard it in a private reserve within the Anjozorobe Forest , a protected corridor of primary and secondary rainforest a couple of hours ' drive north of Tana . So close were we to the hollering indri that we saw the delicate pink of their mouths and gums as they let rip . Later , as night fell , the mouse lemurs that lived in the thatch of our lodge came out one by one -- each a pair of big eyes on a furry stem of a body -- and parachuted in to the undergrowth along thin , bending branches , like firemen down a pole . <p> The mouse and the indri are the little and large of the genus Lemur , labelled prosimians -- primitive monkeys -- by biologists . Lemurs probably arrived from mainland Africa , perhaps on floating vegetation , after Madagascar had broken away in the geological tumult that produced the Earth 's continental land masses . <p> In the words of David Attenborough , the island of Madagascar became " an evolutionary cauldron , producing increasingly extreme forms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an astonishing and imaginative degree that some species were until recently thought to be other animals entirely , while new ones are still being discovered . <p> They are found all over Madagascar , many species confined to discrete terrains and climatic zones , all compellingly unique . Ranging from the frankly bizarre aye-aye , with its bony Dickensian fingers , to the comical diademed sifaka , with its bouncing balletic walk , all the species are under threat to greater or lesser extents . <p> Travelling north , south and then east from Tana , we understood why . The varied landscapes of Madagascar are wonderfully dramatic -- the baobab trees of the west , which look like cigars surmounted by fright wigs , the " spiny forest " adapted to the poor soil and low rainfall of the south . But all face degradation . <p> In the east , where lowland and montane rainforest provides the habitat for several lemur species , many hills now resemble scalps with alopecia , and in some cases the deforestation has resulted in landslips that leave scars of red earth on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by the dreaded eucalyptus , in and below which nothing of value grows . <p> There are several villains , including logging interests . The giant rosewoods of the Masoala peninsula , in the north-east , are being felled " illegally " and sold to the Chinese at a rate of knots , as my guide had witnessed two months previously -- " I wanted to cry , " he said . But the biggest problem is the traditional practice of tavy -- slash-and-burn agriculture . <p> As they have done for thousands of years , the rural poor hack down the rainforest and burn it off , planting their staples of rice and manioc in its place . In the afternoons , when the wind gets up , smoke subsumes the surrounding landscape and even blows across the capital city , casting a strange aquarium light over the coloured rooftops . Madagascar 's future is going up in smoke . <p> The president-before-last , Marc Ravalomanana , recognised this and instituted an ambitious conservation programme aimed at tripling protected areas and providing alternatives to slash-and-burn . But he was ousted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the island 's precious and fragile biodiversity seems to have disappeared down the river , along with the rosewood logs . <p> Madagascar 's continuing unstable political situation is cited as a reason not to go there , but that is nonsense . There is no conceivable danger , or even inconvenience , posed to foreign tourists . The infrastructure is in place -- excellent safari lodges and city hotels , impressive , French-influenced food , informed and impassioned guides -- and the place is crying out for us . Animal-loving , camera-toting vazahas ( foreigners ) represent a vital strand of a sustainable future . <p> The only sign I saw of unrest were the two army trucks parked outside Tana 's Belle ? poque railway station ( now a shopping arcade ) at the end of the Avenue de l'Ind ? pendance . Ignoring the army presence , Tana 's beau monde walks past the trucks to reach the restored Caf ? de la Gare for cr ? pes and cocktails . The soldiers playing cards in the trucks are there apparently to deter demonstrations -- a shame that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the lives of their country 's impoverished population . <p> The extent of that poverty becomes clear as you travel around . Many villages have no electricity or running water , roads are poor , schools are pitifully equipped . Among the fishing-and-farming communities of Lake Ampitabe on the east coast , the adult literacy rate is just 40 per cent and villagers are burning the forests to plant rice and crops because they know no better . <p> The local school , at Ampahantany , battles valiantly to educate the children for a more sustainable future . " Help from the government ? " The white-coated headmaster , who lives in a thatched hut near the two classrooms , shook his head sadly . " No help . " He is paid from handouts by parents . Books are falling apart , latrines have no water , children have little but scabies . <p> But life in Madagascar is also vibrant and cheerful , with sudden splashes of colour and drama -- a riverbank patched with drying washing ; a crocodile of blue-smocked schoolchildren on their way to classes vertes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lavender blue of the jacaranda , a spectral colour that seems to hover in the branches ; the white of an egret against the green of a rice field , as elegant as a single stroke of calligraphy . <p> At the end of each journey comes the anticipation of seeing yet another extraordinary creature that exists nowhere but here . Arriving near Andasibe , home of the indri , we spent the first evening on a night walk along the road bordering the park , picking out by torchlight an eastern dwarf lemur and some eastern woollies -- cuddly sleepyheads in National Health specs -- and finding curled on branches tiny chameleons as exquisite as semi-precious brooches . <p> One day , we took a trip to what may be the future . Vakona Island , popularly known as Lemur Island , is actually three islands occupying some 20 acres in a lake and providing sanctuary for lemurs rescued from the dire fate of being pets . Though the lemurs live freely here , the flora , mostly eucalyptus , is incapable of supporting them so they are fed by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The tourists are punted across in canoes -- and set upon by the tame lemurs , which have the prosimian equivalent of dollar signs in their eyes ( banana signs , perhaps , like fruit-machine symbols ) . The star of the show was undoubtedly a solitary diademed sifaka , which over and over again demonstrated for the cameras her slow-motion , bounding gait . <p> It was comical but disturbing , for as soon as the focus moved away from her she looked confused and bereft . Will this be the only way to view lemurs in 50 years ' time -- in ersatz rainforest , armed with titbits ? <p> It was this prospect that lent the encounter with the indri -- a family in their natural habitat -- such poignancy . The four of them bundled and dawdled about for 20 minutes above our heads until the troupe of humans observing them had grown to 18 . We engaged in some rough-and-tumble behaviour ourselves -- nudging one another out of the way to get better views -- before lapsing into stillness , with nothing left to do but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ignored us . They are used to seeing humans and they behaved as if we did n't exist . Which , for the lemur , as for much of the world 's wildlife , would be the best thing . <p> Nigel Richardson travelled with Aardvark Safaris ( 01980 849160 ; aardvarksafaris.co.uk ) . A two-week trip , including rainforest lodges and wildlife viewing , a homestay in a village and three nights on the island of Sainte Marie , costs from ? 3,815 per person , sharing . The price includes international flights via Paris , internal flights , transfers , English-speaking guide , half-board accommodation and activities . 
@@941904 <h> Song of the suicide bomber : How ' Babur in London ' negotiated a cultural minefield <h> Song of the suicide bomber : How ' Babur in London ' negotiated a cultural minefield <h> 1/3 <p> Cries of pain : Kishani Jayasinghe as Nafisa <p> Brigitte Faessler <h> 2/3 <p> Omar Ebrahim as Babur <p> Brigitte Faessler <h> 3/3 <p> Amar Muchhala as Mo <p> Brigitte Faessler <p> Cries of pain : Kishani Jayasinghe as Nafisa <p> " My name is Mo , short for Mohammed , / I 'm a second-generation immigrant clich ? : Twenty-eight , disenfranchised , well educated. / My mother is white , but I 'm all Paki. / My father owns a corner shop where I work , / And I owe my allegiance to global Umma. / My nation 's the Republic of Islam . " <p> So sings Mo , one of four aspiring suicide bombers in the cast of Babur in London , a daring new opera that tackles modern-day terrorism as well as the legacy of ancient Indian history in its libretto . The production is named @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in India in the 16th century , and who was a famously brutal warlord as well as a brilliant poet . <p> The chamber piece , which begins its UK tour on 12 June after opening in Zurich earlier this year , follows the lives of four radicalised British-Asian characters from a London suburb -- Mo , Faiz , Nafisa and Saira -- as they meet the ghostly spectre of Babur , debate with him such concepts as Jihad , war , the afterlife and the Islamic ban on alcohol , and finally carry out their deadly attack . Some characters wear Western dress while others are in traditional Islamic attire including the hijab , and the stage set is a symbolic , debris-strewn landscape of disused water bottles . <p> Jeet Thayil , the Indian poet and musician , had long wanted to bring the legendary figure of Babur back to life for modern audiences , but it was when he discussed the project with the composer Edward Rushton and director John Fulljames -- soon after the Mumbai terror attacks -- that they decided to incorporate the theme of suicide @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ aware of political sensitivities and the danger of sensationalism so they entered into lengthy discussions with scholars of Islam as well as the counter-terrorism think tank Quilliam Foundation , before they drafted singers and embarked on rehearsals . There will also be a series of accompanying workshops in some cities including London and Bradford which will explore some of the questions that the opera raises , as well as pre- and post-show debates . <p> The opera signals a growing readiness for artists to handle the incendiary theme of contemporary terrorism in their work . It follows a recent , critically feted debut novel Ours Are the Streets , by the British Asian author Sunjeev Sahota , about a would-be suicide bomber who is writing a diary as a final love letter to his wife -- a white British convert to Islam -- and his child , as he prepares to die in the name of Islamic Jihad . Meanwhile , Chris Morris 's film satire Four Lions , featuring a group of bungling suicide bombers , won praise from Moazzam Begg , a former detainee at Guantanamo Bay . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was a legitimate subject for opera to handle , and dismissed the idea that the production might offend sensibilities in Britain , in spite of the London terror attacks which claimed 52 lives in 2005 . " The headline on the tin inevitably has something sensational about it ... and in dealing with a subject like this , we were anxious about audience response but in the end , we talked in detail about what the piece was , and what became clear when we premiered it in Switzerland was that it was seen as a beautiful , thoughtful , delicate piece of work , with nothing sensational about it . " <p> The production is a collaboration between Opera North and The Opera Group ( TOG ) ; the latter has been responsible for previous controversial operas featuring dementia and climate change as their main themes -- they staged The Lion 's Face in 2010 which explored the emotional and physical impact of Alzheimer 's disease , and a year later , Seven Angels , which drew inspiration from Milton 's Paradise Lost to reflect on environmental dangers . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ TOG , says he passionately believes that opera should be part of the contemporary world , and so should not avoid grappling with current-day themes . " I think opera needs to be part of the cultural landscape . The conversation around it should be the same conversation we are having in our cities . If we only make opera about martians who live on the moon , then it 's deeply unsatisfactory . <p> " What opera offers is an emotional space to have these conversations . For example , dementia is very easy to understand as a science , but it 's much harder to understand the emotions . The subject matter here is intellectually -- and emotionally -- challenging for the cast and audience . " <p> Thayil has spent many years reading around the central historical figure in the opera , including Babur 's poetic autobiography , Baburnama , and the Koran , initially read for its poetry -- as a piece of literature -- and later studied seriously when the idea for the libretto started to crystallise in 2005 . <p> The libretto contains highly wrought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but also some combative exchanges when the British youths attempt to argue the case for staging a suicide bombing , while Babur -- a warrior famed for the immense carnage he wreaked -- argues against the practice , saying : " God does n't countenance the slaughter of innocents . " <p> Later on , he declares that these four modern Jihadists are ignorant of the Holy Book , reflecting on the battles he fought which did not include the death of innocents in the way a suicide attack inevitably would : " Suicide is a sin and murder is a sin , / And in the eyes of God you are sinners . " <p> While the themes are morbid and bloodthirsty , there is a rich and complex poetry to the libretto . Thayil says this reflects Babur 's own contradictory spirit . " Poetry and violence are not mutually exclusive , and are in fact married , as a reading of Homer or The Ramayana will prove . "

The new IDS flagship programme , funded by the Department for International Development ( DFID ) , will examine the mutual benefits of collaboration between these countries , as well as the financial , ideological , knowledge , and development assistance footprints of the rising powers in international development . <p> The workshop aimed to develop an innovative , co-constructed agenda for work on the nature , scope and impact of the BRICS countries ' contribution to international development across three themes -- research , teaching and policy engagement -- in order @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at the workshop , IDS Director Lawrence Haddad highlighted the importance of collaboration saying , ' We must work together to solve global issues in a globally constructed forum ' . <h> From recipients to donors : How are the BRICS reshaping international development cooperation ? <p> What do the BRICS think the principles of international development cooperation should be ? How is the international aid architecture changing ? How are practice , learning and teaching , and policy in international development cooperation affected by these changing paradigms ? These were just some of the questions tackled by workshop participants in their discussions examining how the rising powers differ from established donors , and what impact this has on international development . <p> Participants discussed the new and emerging partnerships in , with , and amongst the BRICS and other rising power countries , exploring topics including : <p> South-South Cooperation and how this has been framed by different actors ; <p> the aid-effectiveness versus **25;336;TOOLONG debate ; <p> agriculture , agri-business and the control of natural resources ; <p> the role of business and the private sector in development ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global public goods , such as climate change mitigation . <h> Views from the BRICS <p> Presentations from researchers and policy specialists from the BRICS highlighted common issues across these countries , whether they are creating new relationships with low-income countries or reshaping existing ones . Participants were unanimous in voicing the need for more research on Southern-driven development cooperation . This includes collecting reliable data on the various financial flows not currently classified as development assistance , as well as understanding the new flows of ideas , policies and people . <p> Participants discussed the need to consider broader issues of social justice , while recognising that economic development is currently central to the BRICS development agenda . ' China focuses more on economic collaborations because it realises the value of the BRICS countries and how their relationships can be mutually beneficial ' , said Dr Huo Jianguo , President of the Chinese Academy of International Trade and Economic Cooperation ( CAITEC ) . <p> Dr Sachin Chaturvedi , Senior Fellow at Research and Information Systems for Developing Countries ( RIS ) in India echoed this , ' The BRICS @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ just giving hand-outs , but are also bringing and generating economic activities such as business . ' <p> The workshop identified potential collaborations in a number of fields , including agriculture , health , trade and investment , global public goods , private sector development and social policy . As an overarching theme , it also highlighted the value of learning from the BRICS ' successful policy innovations and exploring their potential contribution to low-income countries . 
@@942068 <p> A stash of 80-year-old photo plates in a Danish basement has proved that Greenland 's ice was melting even faster then that it is now . <p> In the thirties , Greenland 's ice was melting rapidly , then there was a cooling period in the middle part of the twentieth century , and now it is melting again , accelerating in the 2000s . <p> Images of ice shelves from the pre-satellite era are extremely rare , so it 's often difficult to assess the scale and speed of Arctic ice melting today . <p> Researchers at the National Survey and Cadastre of Denmark had been storing the glass plates since explorer Knud Rasmussen 's expedition to the southeast coast of Greenland in the early 1930s . <p> In this week 's online edition of Nature Geoscience , Ohio State University researchers and colleagues in Denmark describe how they analyzed ice loss in the region by comparing the images on the plates to aerial photographs and satellite images taken from World War II to today . <p> Taken together , the imagery shows that glaciers in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they are today , said Jason Box , associate professor of geography and researcher at the Byrd Polar Research Center at Ohio State . <p> A brief cooling period starting in the mid-20th century allowed new ice to form , and then the melting began to accelerate again in the 2000s . <p> ' Because of this study , we now have a detailed historical analogue for more recent glacier loss , ' Box said . ' And we 've confirmed that glaciers are very sensitive indicators of climate . ' <p> Pre-satellite observations of Greenland glaciers are rare - but some are available . <p> Images recorded in 1933 ( left ) and 2010 from a glacier northeast of Tasiilaq : During the 77 year period the glacier has retreated 1.7km <p> Anders Anker Bj ? rk , doctoral fellow at the Natural History Museum of Denmark and lead author of the study , is trying to compile all such imagery . He found a clue in the archives of The Arctic Institute in Copenhagen in 2011 . <p> ' We found flight journals for some old planes , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Denmark , ' Bj ? rk said . <p> A stash of 80-year-old photo plates in a Danish basement has proved that Greenland 's ice was melting even faster then that it is now <p> As it happens , researchers at the National Survey had already contacted Bj ? rk about a find of their own . <p> ' They were cleaning up in the basement and had found some old glass plates with glaciers on them . The reason the plates were forgotten was that they were recorded for mapping , and once the map was produced they did n't have much value . ' <p> Those plates turned out to be documentation of Rasmussen 's 7th Thule Expedition to Greenland . They contained aerial photographs of land , sea and glaciers in the southeast region of the country , along with travel photos of Rasmussen 's team . <p> The researchers digitized all the old images and used software to look for differences in the shape of the southeast Greenland coastline where the ice meets the Atlantic Ocean . <p> Then they calculated the distance the ice front @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ after returning from a surveying mission during Knud Rasmussen ? s expedition to Greenland in the 1930s <p> Over the 80 years , two events stand out : glacial retreats from 1933-1934 and 2000-2010 . In the 1930s , fewer glaciers were melting than are today , and most of those that were melting were land-terminating glaciers , meaning that they did not contact the sea . <p> Those that were melting retreated an average of 20 meters per year - the fastest retreating at 374 meters per year . <p> Fifty-five percent of the glaciers in the study had similar or higher retreat rates during the 1930s than they do today . <p> Still , more glaciers in southeast Greenland are retreating today , and the average ice loss is 50 meters per year . <p> That 's because a few glaciers with very fast melting rates - including one retreating at 887 meters per year - boost the overall average . <p> But to Box , the most interesting part of the study is what happened between the two melting events . <p> From 1943-1972 , southeast Greenland cooled @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ away from the earth . <p> Sulfur dioxide is a poisonous gas produced by volcanoes and industrial processes . It has been tied to serious health problems and death , and is also the main ingredient in acid rain . Its presence in the atmosphere peaked just after the Clean Air Act was established in 1963 . As it was removed from the atmosphere , the earlier warming resumed . <p> The important point is not that deadly pollution caused the climate to cool , but rather that the brief cooling allowed researchers to see how Greenland ice responded to the changing climate . <p> The glaciers responded to the cooling more rapidly than researchers had seen in earlier studies . Sixty percent of the glaciers advanced during that time , while 12 percent were stationary . And now that the warming has resumed , the glacial retreat is dominated by marine-terminating outlet glaciers , the melting of which contributes to sea level rise . <p> ' From these images , we see that the mid-century cooling stabilized the glaciers , ' Box said . ' That suggests that if we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ need to see a little cooling of our own . ' <p> Southeast Greenland is a good place to study the effects of climate change , he explained , because the region is closely tied to air and water circulation patterns in the North Atlantic . <p> ' By far , more storms pass through this region - transporting heat into the Arctic - than anywhere else in the Northern Hemisphere . Climate change brings changes in snowfall and air temperature that compete for influence on a glacier 's net behavior , ' he said .
